The read everything is built on
Tap That Brewery is a refill subscription business being run with a hospitality venue's attention.

220 active keg customers against a 1,000 target. Systems go out at cost and all the margin sits in refills — but eighteen months of attention went to the taproom, which loses money and was only ever meant to be the shopfront. Half your refill customers already switched to you from another supplier with no campaign at all.

Everything downstream follows from that. For the short version of this pack — what's here, what isn't, and what's blocking sign-off — see the delivery summary.

Status: every document is a working draft at v01, AI-assisted, and none are approved. The Design phase was written ahead of Discover sign-off so the whole shape could be seen at once — each Design document says so on its face. Where a figure is an estimate, a single source or an open question, it is marked rather than smoothed over.

Deal Memo
Document 01 — Deal Memo (Maxxim catalogue #1, Gate 0 — Onboarding)
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 00 Admin — Gate 0 (Onboarding)
Status Draft v01 — Internal (Jewell only, candid)
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects

Purpose

Internal record of why this deal is worth doing, what shape it should take, and what could go wrong. Written after the 19 August discovery session and site visit; before proposal issue. Not for client eyes.

Who the client is

Tap That Brewery, Burleigh Heads (industrial area, Gold Coast). Founders: Chris Smith (head brewer — ex Burleigh Barrels, the product credibility; Brewer's Choice at Crafted for Midnight in Tokyo in their first year entering) and Justin Mistry (physio since 2008, Back in Motion franchise background — the business model and the 10–12-year franchise ambition). One hybrid marketer, Raef (~70% marketing / 30% bar cover).

Four business units: keg system sales (hardware sold at cost, $975 two-tap to $2,550 six-tap), 20L keg refills (the margin engine — ~$200/month per active customer, ~2 kegs), a 27-tap taproom (~1/3 non-beer by design; loss-making but converts 20–30% of visitors into system buyers), and secondary wholesale. Stack: GoTab POS, Fishbowl CRM (integration pending), Buffer-ready socials, Afterpay/Zip.

The commercial problem

220 active refill customers against a 1,000 target — ~$2.6M at ~$200/month — while the business runs at a loss ("always red at the bottom of the P&L," Justin's words). The last 18 months drifted into growing the taproom, which was only ever meant to be the shopfront for the refill subscription business.

The critical nuance from discovery: this is not a strategy gap. The founders' mental model is right ("everything feeds refill volume"), the five pillars are printed on in-venue collateral, the segments are mapped, the offers exist and are photo-verified. What they lack is execution capacity — one part-time marketer cannot run segment campaigns, wholesale outreach, lifecycle automation, UGC, and events simultaneously. The $2.6M target currently has no delivery mechanism. That is the job.

Why Jewell Projects is a fit

  1. They need sequencing and production, not ideation. The 3D Process is exactly this: turn a validated diagnosis (D06 already drafted) into a strategy the client signs, then into briefs and assets a 0.7 FTE marketer can actually run.
  2. The unit economics reward strategy work. Every refill customer is ~$2,400/year of high-margin recurring revenue; small conversion gains on the giveaway funnel (currently 22–25 systems per cycle) or the switcher channel (50% of refill customers already came from elsewhere, unprompted) move real money.
  3. Discovery is substantially done. Session, site visit, 27 photos, verified pricing, four Discover anchor drafts (D02, D03, D05, D06) — we enter with momentum and low cost-to-serve on Phase 1.
  4. The franchise horizon means the strategy work compounds: what we systemise now becomes the replicable playbook Justin needs in 10–12 years — and a strong JP case study either way.

Engagement risks (candid)

Risk Read Mitigation
Execution bottleneck is structural Raef at ~70% is the whole delivery capacity; a brilliant strategy can die in his inbox Scope Design outputs to what 0.7 FTE can run; make the Deploy execution retainer the honest answer, and say so in the proposal
Taproom gravity 18 months of drift shows the venue keeps winning founder attention because it is visible and social; it may pull our engagement the same way Hold the line in every gate: refills first, systems second, taproom as funnel, wholesale as opportunism
Data gaps at the base Census is headline-only (n=50), P&L detail is "red at the bottom," two live membership posters disagree on price ($250/yr vs $300+$120 renewal), paddle signed $18 vs $20 on screen, competitor name unverified ("Aardvark & Arrow"?) Gate 1 sign-off blocked until census lands and pricing is reconciled; do not build funnel copy on contaminated numbers
Loss-making client Fee tolerance is untested; a client bleeding cash may baulk mid-engagement Price Discover–Design as a fixed scope with CP1/CP2 exits; tie Deploy fees to the refill growth narrative
Automation dependency Lifecycle work (H4) is blocked on GoTab/Fishbowl integration outside our control Get the integration timeline in writing before CP2 sequences CRM deliverables
Two-founder dynamic Chris owns product, Justin owns model; sign-off authority not yet explicit Name a single sign-off holder in the Welcome Pack; confirm at kick-off

Scope shape

Recommended: 3D Process through Design, closing at CP2, with Deploy execution scoped separately once the roadmap is approved.

  • Discover (largely complete): finalise D02/D03/D05/D06 with census data, produce D07 readout + D08 Gate 1 deck → CP1 sign-off on the Discover Summary.
  • Design: the Maxxim catalogue #23–64 — business plan through brand strategy, marketing plan, channel plans, and the brief pack — culminating in the Strategy Summary → CP2 sign-off and the Approved Roadmap.
  • Deploy (subsequent engagement): execution against the roadmap — the honest answer to the capacity constraint. Deliberately not priced or committed until CP2, so the client buys a roadmap they have already approved.

Open commercial questions

  1. Fees and payment structure for Discover–Design — fixed fee per phase vs single engagement fee; the client's loss position argues for phase-gated commitment.
  2. Deploy model — retainer (fractional marketing team around Raef) vs project builds; raise at CP2, seed the expectation now.
  3. Who signs — Justin, Chris, or both? One name needed on the Proposal.
  4. IP and case-study rights — the franchise playbook angle makes Blueprint Extraction unusually valuable; secure case-study consent in the SOW.
  5. Does the scope include creative production (photo/video/UGC direction implies shoots) or briefs only?
  6. Timeline pressure — next giveaway cycle and the booked September Brazilian night are natural deadlines; do we anchor Design delivery to either?

Assumptions & open items

  • Segment percentages, census detail, membership pricing, paddle price, and competitor identity all carry the pending flags from D06 — nothing here relies on them resolving a particular way.
  • "Loss-making" is founder-stated; no P&L has been sighted.
  • Franchise ambition (10–12 years) is founder intent, not a plan.

Feeds into: 02 Proposal (client-facing), 03 Welcome Pack, and the SOW/pricing work in 00 Admin/08 Proposal + Pricing.

Proposal
Document 02 — Proposal (Maxxim catalogue #2, Gate 0 — Onboarding) · Client sign-off document
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 00 Admin — Gate 0 (Onboarding)
Status Draft v01 — Client-facing
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects

Where you are

You told us plainly, and we heard it: the business is about connection and good times — keg systems and refills are the means, not the end. The model is proven. Systems go out the door at cost ($975 for a two-tap), and the business lives on 20L refills — around $200 a month from every active customer. You have 220 active keg customers today and a target of 1,000, which is roughly a $2.6M business. Half your refill customers bought their hardware somewhere else and switched to you anyway. The taproom converts 20–30% of visitors into system owners. Midnight in Tokyo took Brewer's Choice at Crafted in your first year entering.

And yet, in your own words, it's "always red at the bottom of the P&L." The last 18 months pulled focus toward the taproom, and the refill engine — where the margin actually lives — grew on word of mouth rather than a system.

Here's the thing we want to say clearly, because it's rarer than you'd think: you don't have a strategy problem. You know your segments, your pillars, your objections, your opportunities. What's missing is the machine to execute it — with one marketer at roughly 70% capacity, segment campaigns, re-engagement, wholesale follow-up and content can't all happen at once. That's the problem this engagement solves.

How we work: the 3D Process

Our engagements run through three phases, each closed by a checkpoint you sign before we move on:

Phase What it does Checkpoint
Discover Establish the validated facts: audience, competitors, offers, economics, and the priority problems CP1 — you sign the Discover Summary
Design Turn the diagnosis into the strategy: brand, messaging, plans by channel, and production-ready briefs CP2 — you sign the Strategy Summary and Approved Roadmap
Deploy Execute the roadmap: builds, launches, automation, content, reporting Scoped and agreed at CP2

Nothing advances past a checkpoint without your sign-off, and nothing in Deploy gets built that wasn't approved at CP2. You always know what you've agreed to and what comes next.

What we'll deliver, phase by phase

Discover (well progressed — the 19 August session and site visit did the heavy lifting). We complete the Discover document set: audience teardown, competitor analysis, offer worksheet with your full photo-verified pricing, and the Discovery Summary that pulls it into one agreed picture — closing with a readout presentation at CP1. This is where we lock the facts: the 220→1,000 goal, the 75/90-day customer lifecycle, the four buying objections and your answers to them, and the six priority problems to solve.

Design. The full strategy layer, built only on what you signed at CP1: business plan and growth roadmap; customer profiles and a journey mapped to your real 3-month buying cycle; brand strategy, positioning and messaging built on your five pillars (cost, convenience, connection, kudos, taste/freshness) and your existing voice and identity — we document and sharpen what's working, we don't reinvent it; website, SEO, social and content strategy; the in-market activation plan covering the switcher opportunity, giveaways, the Benchy, sports clubs and partnerships; channel plans (paid, email, CRM, PR, events); and a complete brief pack so every asset can be produced without re-explaining the business. It closes with the Strategy Summary and Approved Roadmap at CP2.

Deploy. Execution of the approved roadmap. Because the honest constraint in this business is hands, not ideas, Deploy is where Jewell Projects becomes the delivery capacity around Raef — builds, launches, lifecycle automation once GoTab/Fishbowl connect, content production and reporting. We scope and price Deploy at CP2, when you can see exactly what you're buying.

The outcomes this is built for

Everything above serves one number — 1,000 active refill customers — through four levers we already see in your data:

  1. Switchers: 50% of your refill base came from systems bought elsewhere with zero targeted effort. A deliberate "we fill any system" lane is your cheapest growth.
  2. Conversion: the giveaway funnel (about 1,000 entries, twice a year, 22–25 systems per cycle) and the taproom's 20–30% conversion, nurtured properly across the 3-month buying cycle instead of left to memory.
  3. Retention: automated lifecycle contact at 60/75/90 days so at-risk customers are caught before they lapse, replacing the manual monthly SMS.
  4. New pools: Benchy (no direct competitor), sports club white-label, outdoor-living partnerships, and delivery via GoTab × Uber Direct.

Ways of working

  • Checkpoints: CP1 closes Discover, CP2 closes Design. Each is a working session plus a sign-off document — your formal approval that the phase is right and the next may begin.
  • Sign-off: a named decision-maker on your side approves each checkpoint document in writing. Signed means locked; changes after sign-off are handled as revisions, not relitigation.
  • Cadence: a short weekly check-in during active phases, with Raef in the loop as the operator; founders needed mainly at checkpoints and for the inputs below.
  • One source of truth: every document lives in a shared, structured engagement folder — no version confusion.

What we need from you

Input Why it matters When
Full census data (the n=50 survey) + supporting business docs Validates segment estimates and objection responses before we build messaging on them Now — carried from the discovery session (Clent → Jules, Google Drive)
Membership pricing reconciliation Two posters are live with different Keg Crew pricing ($250/yr vs $300 + $120 renewal) and token values; also the tasting paddle ($18 sign vs $20 screen). We need the real numbers before any pricing copy Before CP1
Competitor confirmation Correct name and details for the brewery across the road Before CP1
GoTab/Fishbowl integration timeline Sequences the CRM and lifecycle automation work Before CP2
Founder time CP1 and CP2 sessions, plus a small number of working inputs (P&L shape, franchise definition, lease-to-buy terms) Scheduled at kick-off
A named sign-off holder One person whose signature closes each checkpoint At kick-off

Welcome

Cheers — we're glad to be aboard. This pack is the practical companion to the Proposal: who's who, how the engagement runs day to day, what happens when, and the short list of things we need from you to get moving. Keep it handy; it answers most "how does this work?" questions.

One line on why we're here, in your words: you're in the business of connection and good times — keg systems and refills are the means, not the end. Our job is to build the marketing machine that takes you from 220 active keg customers to 1,000.

Who's who

Your side

Person Role In this engagement
Justin Mistry Co-founder, operator Business model, commercial decisions, franchise vision; checkpoint sessions
Chris Smith Co-founder, head brewer Product, range and brewing credibility; input on brand and product marketing
Raef Marketing — organic social, content, events (~70% marketing / 30% bar) Our day-to-day operator contact; everything we design must be runnable by this seat

We'll confirm the full team list and titles at kick-off, including who holds sign-off (see below).

Our side

Person Role Contact
Clent Jewell Engagement lead — your first call for anything clent@jewellprojects.com
Christy Kilmartin Strategy via Clent
Jules Collateral, photography and data intake — receives your census data and documents via Google Drive via Clent

How the engagement runs

We work in three phases — Discover, Design, Deploy — each closed by a checkpoint you sign before the next begins.

Stage What happens Where it ends
Discover (underway) We lock the validated facts: your audience, competitors, offers, pricing and priority problems. The 19 August session and site visit did most of this CP1 — Discovery readout session; you sign the Discover Summary
Design The full strategy: brand, messaging, customer journey, channel plans and production-ready briefs — built only on what you signed at CP1 CP2 — Strategy presentation; you sign the Strategy Summary and Approved Roadmap
Deploy Execution of the approved roadmap: builds, launches, automation, content, reporting. Scoped and agreed at CP2 Ongoing delivery and review rhythm

What happens next, in order:

  1. Now: you send the checklist items below; we finish the Discover documents.
  2. CP1 (Discovery readout): ~90-minute session with both founders. We present the full picture of the business as we've validated it; you correct anything wrong and sign.
  3. Design phase: weekly check-ins with Raef; founders pulled in for a small number of working inputs.
  4. CP2 (Strategy presentation): the strategy, the roadmap, and the Deploy scope and pricing. You sign, and we start building.

Communication cadence

Rhythm What Who
Weekly (active phases) 20–30 minute check-in: progress, blockers, what we need Clent + Raef (founders optional)
At each checkpoint Working session + sign-off document Founders + Jewell team
Ad hoc Email to clent@jewellprojects.com; we reply within one business day Anyone
Documents Everything lives in the shared engagement folder — nothing important travels only by email or text Both sides

What "sign-off" means

Two documents in this engagement carry a signature: the Discover Summary (CP1) and the Strategy Summary + Approved Roadmap (CP2) — plus the Proposal that starts it all.

  • Signing means: this is accurate, this is agreed, build on it. It protects you — nothing gets designed on facts you haven't confirmed, and nothing gets built you haven't approved.
  • One named person on your side signs (agree who at kick-off — our default suggestion is Justin, with Chris consulted on anything brand or product).
  • After sign-off, a document is locked. New information doesn't reopen it informally — it becomes a versioned revision, so there's always one agreed source of truth.
  • Sign-offs are recorded in the engagement folder under Approvals.

Your checklist — things to send us

# Item Notes Send to
1 Census data — the full n=50 survey responses Carried from the discovery session; headline figures only received so far Jules, via the Google Drive folder
2 Membership pricing — the real numbers Two posters are live in the venue with different Keg Crew pricing ($250/yr vs $300 + $120/yr renewal) and different token values; tell us which is current. Same for the tasting paddle ($18 sign vs $20 screen) Clent
3 Competitor across the road — correct business name We have two spellings on record and want to profile them properly Clent
4 P&L / financials — whatever shape you're comfortable sharing Even a summary sharpens the business plan and roadmap Clent (held confidentially)
5 GoTab/Fishbowl integration timeline Sequences the re-engagement automation work Clent
6 Lease-to-buy status Terms and expected go-live (GoTab-dependent) Clent
7 Brand assets — logo files, fonts, any guidelines You have an identity that works; we build with it, not over it Jules
8 Access (when Design starts) Website admin, Meta Business Suite, GoTab/Fishbowl reporting, Google Business Profile Clent — we'll send a secure request
9 Named sign-off holder One name for CP1/CP2 signatures Confirm at kick-off
10 Dates Next giveaway cycle dates + key events (e.g. the September Brazilian night), so the roadmap lands ahead of them Clent

Items 1–3 are the ones holding up CP1 — everything else can follow.

One ask in return

Tell us early when something changes — a price, a product, a poster, a plan. Small drifts (like two membership posters) become expensive when they reach ad copy. We'd rather hear it in a weekly check-in than find it on a sign.

Audience Teardown

Segments ranked by commercial attractiveness for the keg system → refill model. Percentages are the client's own estimates from the discovery workshop (deliberately overlapping, not summing to 100%). Message pillars: Cost, Convenience, Connection, Kudos, Taste/Freshness.

1. Young families (~45%) — the lead growth pillar

  • Excruciating pain: going out is dead — babysitters, cost, logistics. Social life must come to the backyard, and hosting well is effortful and expensive.
  • Before → after: from carting warm slabs and clutter to "the house the mates default to" — beer on tap, seltzers for the partners, everyone stays connected without leaving home.
  • Purchasing power: solid but scrutinised; the $975 outlay is a household decision → the "my wife won't let me" objection lives here, answered by the 1/3 non-beer tap range (her drinks on tap too) and RTD/cocktail options.
  • Message emphasis: Connection first, cost as the quiet justification ("cheaper than a night out, and nobody needs a babysitter").
  • Reachability: Meta, local community groups, school/sport networks, giveaway entries.
  • Guardrail: this pillar must not swallow the brand — Tap That Brewery is good times for everyone, not "the dad brand."

2. High-volume drinkers (~20%) — the economics segment

  • Pain: the weekly bottle-shop spend is genuinely painful at their volume; pub prices worse.
  • Before → after: from $100+ weekends to ~$120–170 per 20L keg (44 schooners) at home.
  • Message emphasis: the one segment where cost + convenience should lead openly. Do the maths for them.
  • Watch-out: the census counters the excess narrative (70% daily users drink only 1–2 kegs/month) — market moderation-friendly ("fresher, better, not more").

3. Cashed-up tradies (~20%) — the kudos build

  • Pain: the shed/man-cave/outdoor build is a status project that's never finished; a fridge full of tins doesn't impress anyone.
  • Before → after: from esky to envy — a plumbed multi-tap system is the crown of the build.
  • Message emphasis: Kudos + connection. Show the build, the mates' reaction, the pour.
  • Reachability: 4WD/outdoor culture (Benchy crossover), trade social channels, UGC of installs. Afterpay/Zip matter here.

4. Baby boomers (~20%) — the quality entertainers

  • Pain: entertain family often, care about quality and hosting generously without fuss.
  • Before → after: from "grandad's stubbies" to fresh 20L variety that makes their place the family hub.
  • Message emphasis: Connection; taste/freshness. The 5L birthday keg and membership perks resonate.

Secondary segments

Segment Est. Emphasis Note
Share houses ~5% Kudos + cost Split four ways the system is cheap; party utility
DINKs ~5% Kudos + taste/freshness Premium/top-shelf range, cocktails on tap
Workplaces ~5% All pillars Friday knock-offs; keg system in the office; functions
"Cashed-up wankers" ~5% Kudos Status buyers — top-shelf range, integrated systems ($1,750+)

The cross-cutting audience: the partner / female market

Explicitly engineered for in the tap range (1/3 non-beer: seltzers, RTDs, gin sours, cocktails, ginger beer). Not a purchase segment so much as the veto-holder on the #1 objection. Every campaign to segments 1, 3, and 4 needs a "and here's what's on tap for her" beat. Health angle (gluten-reduced, lower-carb) is unmarketed ammunition here.

The other funnel: pre-existing keg system owners

Not a demographic — a behavioural segment, and the cheapest acquisition available: owners of any brand of system who need refills. 50% of current refill customers already came this way organically, with zero targeted effort. "We fill any system" deserves its own campaign lane.

Feeds into: DS01 Customer Profile (PAIN/GAIN/PROOF), DS02 Customer Journey, D06 synthesis. Pending: census detail to validate estimates.

Customer Segments
Document Customer Segments — catalogue #05
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects

How to read this model

Share estimates are the client's own, given in the discovery workshop, and are deliberately overlapping — they do not sum to 100%. A cashed-up tradie with young kids is counted twice, and that is the point: segments here are message lenses, not exclusive boxes. Every segment converges on the same commercial spine — system at cost ($975–$2,550) → ~$200/month in 20L refills — so ranking is by which lens produces refill customers fastest and cheapest, not by demographic size alone.

Pillars: Cost · Convenience · Connection · Kudos · Taste/Freshness. Working rule from discovery (Christy): cost is the customer's dirty little secret — kudos/connection lead the creative, cost closes.

Ranked demographic segments

# Segment Share (est.) Pillar emphasis Why this rank
1 Young families ~45% Connection (cost closes) Biggest pool, strongest pain, best fit with the 27-tap range
2 High-volume drinkers ~20% Cost + convenience (lead openly) Fastest payback maths; highest refill cadence per customer
3 Cashed-up tradies ~20% Kudos + connection Multi-tap upsell ($1,950–$2,550), UGC engine, Benchy crossover
4 Baby boomers ~20% Connection; taste/freshness Reliable but slower; strong membership fit
5 Workplaces ~5% All pillars Small but one sale = many drinkers; feeds functions revenue
6 DINKs ~5% Kudos + taste/freshness Top-shelf and cocktail range buyers
7 Share houses ~5% Kudos + cost $975 ÷ 4 housemates is trivial; churn risk when houses dissolve
8 "Cashed-up wankers" ~5% Kudos Status buyers; integrated systems from $1,750

1. Young families (~45%)

On the Gold Coast this is the Burleigh–Palm Beach–Miami–Tugun belt: mortgaged 30s–40s households where the backyard, not the venue strip, is the social unit. Babysitters plus a night out in Burleigh costs more than a keg; the school-and-nippers network is the word-of-mouth channel. The purchase is a household decision — see the partner overlay below. Creative leads with connection ("your place becomes the default"); cost is the quiet justification in retargeting. Guardrail from D02: this segment must not turn Tap That Brewery into "the dad brand."

2. High-volume drinkers (~20%)

The one segment where the maths leads openly: a 20L keg is 44 schooners for $120–$170 (member core $120) against a $100+ bottle-shop weekend. Convenience seals it — the drive to Dan Murphy's disappears. Watch-out: the census (n=50) shows 70% of daily users drink only 1–2 kegs/month, so creative stays "fresher and cheaper, not more" — never a bulk-drinking pitch.

3. Cashed-up tradies (~20%)

Gold Coast trade money with a shed, a ute and an unfinished outdoor build. The keg system is the crown of the man-cave — this is where 4-tap ($1,950) and 6-tap ($2,550) sales live, and where Afterpay/Zip matter most. Reachable through 4WD/outdoor culture (the Benchy portable tap, Raef's cousin's 4WD fit-out network) and install UGC. Kudos leads; the mates' reaction is the ad.

4. Baby boomers (~20%)

Established southern-GC households hosting kids and grandkids. Quality entertainers: taste/freshness and generous, fuss-free hosting. Best natural fit for Keg Crew membership ($250/yr, $30 off refills, 5L birthday keg) and the corny-keg service relationship. Slower buying cycle; lowest creative-risk segment.

5–8. Secondary segments (~5% each)

Workplaces (Friday knock-offs in Burleigh's industrial estate — the taproom's own neighbours are the prospect list), DINKs (premium range, cocktails on tap), share houses (split cost, party utility, address churn), and status buyers (top-shelf, integrated systems). None warrants its own campaign yet; each is a variant of an existing lane.

Behavioural overlay 1 — the partner / veto-holder

Not a purchase segment: the approval audience sitting across segments 1, 3 and 4, and the source of the #1 objection ("my wife won't let me"). Tap That Brewery has already engineered the answer into the product — roughly a third of 27 taps are non-beer (seltzers, Strawberry Gin Fizz sour, ginger beer, Ranch Water, mojitos on tap). Every campaign to the top segments needs a "here's what's on tap for her" beat, and the unmarketed gluten-reduced / lower-carb range is held ammunition for this audience. Win the veto-holder and the 3-month buying cycle shortens; ignore her and no amount of retargeting closes it.

Behavioural overlay 2 — pre-existing keg system owners

The cheapest acquisition available and a segment defined by behaviour, not demography: anyone on the Gold Coast who already owns a keg system — whoever sold it to them — and needs it filled monthly. ~50% of current refill customers arrived this way with zero targeted effort, skipping the $975 objection, the partner veto and the 3-month cycle entirely and going straight to the ~$200/month margin engine. No competitor is building this relationship (D03: the category gap). "We fill any system" deserves its own always-on campaign lane, ranked in practice above every demographic segment on cost-per-refill-customer.

Assumptions & open items

  • All share percentages are client estimates from the workshop wall (photo 12-workshop-notes-…); census detail pending and may revise them.
  • Gold Coast geographic colour (suburb belts, trade demographics) is Jewell inference, not client-stated — validate against sales postcodes once GoTab/Fishbowl data lands.
  • Workplace segment sizing assumes the industrial-estate neighbour opportunity flagged for the taproom transfers to system sales — untested.

Feeds into: 06 Customer Pain-Points, 07 Buying Triggers & Barriers, 08 Jobs To Be Done, DS01 Customer Profile, DS02 Customer Journey.

Customer Pain Points
Document Customer Pain-Points — catalogue #06
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects

Why this document exists

Copy that names the customer's pain in the customer's words outperforms copy that describes the product. This document banks the pains per segment (pre-purchase) and per lifecycle stage (post-purchase) so Design-phase creative pulls lines from here rather than inventing them. Phrases in quotes are the voice of the customer — drawn from the discovery session's stated objections and segment logic, written the way the segment would say them.

Pre-purchase pains by segment

Segment The pain, in their words Underneath it Pillar that answers it
Young families "Going out is dead — by the time we've paid a sitter and Ubers, it's $300 before the first beer." Social life must relocate to the backyard, but hosting well is effortful and expensive — warm slabs, ice runs, esky clutter Connection (cost closes)
Young families "We host every weekend and I spend Saturday morning at the bottle-o." Hosting logistics tax; nothing for the partners to drink but wine Convenience; the non-beer taps
High-volume drinkers "My bottle-shop spend is embarrassing — $100+ every weekend and it's still warm tins." Volume economics: pub prices worse, packaged beer stale next to fresh 20L Cost + convenience
Cashed-up tradies "The shed's 90% done — has been for two years." The man-cave/outdoor build is a status project with no crown; a fridge of tins impresses nobody Kudos
Cashed-up tradies "Mates come round and I'm handing out cans like it's a worksite." Hosting doesn't match the money spent on the build Kudos + connection
Baby boomers "When the family's over I want it done properly, without me running around all afternoon." Generous hosting is identity; fuss and mediocre beer undermine it Connection; taste/freshness
Share houses "Four of us, one fridge, everyone's beer goes missing." Party utility without individual expense; splitting $975 four ways is easy Kudos + cost
DINKs "We'd rather two great drinks than a fridge of average ones." Quality signalling; cocktails/top-shelf at home Kudos + taste/freshness
Workplaces "Friday knock-offs are a trip to the bottle-o and warm cups in the car park." Team ritual with no infrastructure Connection + convenience
Partner / veto-holder "It's a thousand dollars for his beer." Sees a beer toy, not a household asset; nothing in it for her The 27-tap answer: ~1/3 non-beer — seltzers, gin fizz sour, ginger beer, cocktails on tap
Existing system owners (behavioural) "I've got the kegerator — getting it filled is the annoying part." Commodity swaps, no relationship, stale or inconsistent fills elsewhere Taste/freshness + convenience ("we fill any system")

Two pains recur across every segment and belong in all creative: the night out no longer stacks up (cost, logistics, babysitters, closing time) and hosting at home is effortful — the keg system converts hosting from a chore into a flex.

The four purchase-blocking pains (objections)

Named in discovery as the recurring four; treated fully in document 07:

  1. "A grand up front is a lot for beer." (~$975)
  2. "My wife won't let me." (the veto-holder overlay)
  3. "Nowhere to put it." (space, rental, bench real estate)
  4. "I don't trust myself — I'll drink too much." (countered by census: 70% of daily users go through only 1–2 kegs/month)

Pains of the refill lifecycle (post-purchase)

The margin lives here, so friction here is churn. Lifecycle definitions in use: active <75 days since last keg, at-risk 75–90, churned 90+.

Lifecycle pain In their words Consequence Current / pending answer
Running dry "Mates arrived Saturday and the keg blew halfway through." The system's whole promise fails at the social moment it exists for No low-keg warning today; delivery (GoTab × Uber Direct, same-day capable) and cadence-based prompts post Fishbowl integration
Forgetting to swap "I keep meaning to drop in — it's been two months." The lapsed-not-lost drift: churned customers contacted said they'd "not gotten around to it" Manual monthly SMS ($20 off, 2-week window); automation at 60/75/90 days pending GoTab/Fishbowl
The refill errand "Burleigh's a 25-minute round trip with the empty rattling in the boot." Convenience pillar erodes with distance from the taproom Census says $20–30 delivery fee and 3–7 days acceptable; Uber Direct beats that
Corny keg wait "Drop it Tuesday, get it Friday — so I'm dry half the week." Stainless owners lose days per cycle (+ $3 cleaning fee) The plastic bladder keg: pick-up-and-go, no CO2 — ~70% of corny customers have already switched
Range paralysis / same-again fatigue "I just get the lager because I don't know what's pouring." Refill cadence slows when the range isn't visible at home Tasting notes QR exists in venue; no owned digital "what's kegged & ready" channel yet
Membership ambiguity "Is it $250 a year or $300? What do I actually get?" Two conflicting live posters blunt the strongest retention offer ($30 off every refill) Reconciliation flagged as a production gate in D06

Assumptions & open items

  • Quoted lines are Jewell renderings of client-stated pains, not verbatim customer quotes — validate and harvest real wording via the pending census detail and refill-counter conversations.
  • "Running dry" and "range fatigue" are inferred lifecycle pains; the confirmed churn narrative is "cut back on drinking" or "hadn't gotten around to it."
  • Distance/delivery pain assumes customer postcodes spread beyond Burleigh — confirm from sales data.

Feeds into: 07 Buying Triggers & Barriers, 08 Jobs To Be Done, DS01 Customer Profile (PAIN/GAIN/PROOF), lifecycle messaging design (DS-phase).

Buying Triggers & Barriers
Document Buying Triggers & Barriers — catalogue #07
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects

The shape of the cycle

A keg system is a considered household purchase, not an impulse buy. Client's own figures:

Time to purchase Share of buyers
Within 1 month ~10%
Up to 3 months ~60%
Longer than 3 months ~40%

Working shape: a ~90-day consideration window between first trigger and money down. Inside it, triggers accumulate and barriers get negotiated (usually at home, with the veto-holder). The venue runs this nurture physically — taproom visit → demo → giveaway entry → 50%-off close — but there is no owned digital equivalent: the 3-month window is currently nurtured by memory. That gap is the single biggest conversion fix available and maps directly to hypothesis H3 in D06.

Triggers (what starts or restarts the clock)

Trigger Mechanism Evidence / scale
Taproom visit + demo "That's a keg system?" — seeing it poured in venue collapses abstraction into want 20–30% of taproom visitors go on to buy a system — the venue's real job
Giveaway entry 2×/year keg-system giveaways; entering is a hand-raise that self-identifies in-window buyers ~1,000 entries per round — the largest intent list the business builds
The 50%-off close Post-giveaway offer to entrants who didn't win Converted 22–25 systems last round — the proven closing mechanic
A mate's system Seeing it at a barbecue is the demo without the taproom; referral rewards formalise it 1,000 Tap Tokens per system referral (photo-verified sign); UGC flagged as underexecuted
New outdoor build Deck, pool, outdoor kitchen, man-cave completion — the system is the finishing piece Smoked Barbecue Co Father's Day collab as proof; outdoor-living JVs reach buyers 6–9 months early
Father's Day / gifting moments Household permission moment — the veto-holder becomes the buyer Existing Father's Day keg system + smoking masterclass bundle
Life-stage shift to home hosting First baby, new house: the night out dies and the backyard takes over The ~45% young-families segment logic
Benchy / 4WD-camping trigger Fit-out culture purchase alongside fridges and awnings No direct competitor; warm channel via Raef's cousin's 4WD fit-out business

Sequencing note: triggers stack. The realistic path is mate's place or taproom → giveaway entry → nurture → 50% close or finance — each campaign should aim to advance a buyer one step, not to sell cold.

Barriers — the four objections, current and proposed counters

# Objection (their words) Current counter Proposed / pending counter
1 "A grand up front is a lot." ($975 2-tap) Afterpay + Zip at point of sale (photo-verified); systems honestly framed as sold at cost; $40–$100 bonus credit Lease-to-buy in development (GoTab-dependent, expected within a couple of months) — reframes $975 as a monthly figure below the bottle-shop spend it replaces
2 "My wife won't let me." ~1/3 of 27 taps non-beer by design — seltzers, Strawberry Gin Fizz, ginger beer, Ranch Water, cocktails; "her taps too" Veto-holder-addressed creative (taste the range in the taproom together); market the gluten-reduced / lower-carb angle; gifting-moment campaigns that make her the buyer
3 "Nowhere to put it." 5L mini keg system ($225) as the small-footprint entry; Benchy portable; integrated systems from $1,750 Footprint-proof content: real installs in real Gold Coast garages, benches, sheds; assembly & delivery ($300) removes the DIY fear
4 "I'll drink too much." Anecdotal reassurance ("put a lock on the fridge") Census data (n=50): 70% of daily users go through only 1–2 kegs/month — ownership correlates with moderation, not excess. Fold into objection-handling content; aligns with the sessionable, lower-carb range

Barrier 4's counter is the most under-used asset: it is proprietary data, it is counter-intuitive, and it reassures the veto-holder (barrier 2) at the same time. One piece of content kills two objections.

Implication: the 90-day nurture (proposed, not decided)

Match the machine to the cycle instead of hoping memory holds:

Phase Days Job Content
Spark 0–14 Capture the hand-raise Giveaway entry / demo booking (cheers@); "that's a keg system?" content
Negotiate 15–60 Arm the buyer for the household conversation The four counters, one per touch: finance options, her taps, footprint proof, census moderation stat
Close 61–90 Convert in-window 50%-off close (proven), lease-to-buy once live, Keg Crew membership attached at purchase
Post-90 90+ Don't discard the 40% who take longer Slow lane: seasonal releases, Kegged & Ready range, next giveaway re-entry

Assumptions & open items

  • The 90-day window and nurture phasing are Jewell constructions on the client's 10/60/40 figures — validate against actual GoTab purchase-date data.
  • Lease-to-buy timing and terms unconfirmed ("couple of months", GoTab-dependent) — do not publish until live.
  • Census beyond headline figures pending; the 70% / 1–2 kegs stat must be re-verified from the raw data before it appears in public-facing copy.
  • 50%-off close economics (margin impact on at-cost hardware) not yet examined — confirm the discount applies to hardware and how it is funded.

Feeds into: 08 Jobs To Be Done, DS02 Customer Journey, DS03 Marketing Plan (nurture build), lifecycle automation design.

Jobs To Be Done
Document Jobs To Be Done — catalogue #08
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects

Frame

Nobody hires a keg system for the hardware — the client said it themselves: "we're in the business of connection and good times — keg systems and refills are the means, not the end." This document states what each segment is actually hiring Tap That Brewery to do, in JTBD form (When [situation], help me [job] so that [outcome]), split functional / social / emotional. It then treats the four purchase moments — system, refill, taproom visit, membership — as distinct jobs, because a customer can fire Tap That Brewery from one while still employing it for another.

Jobs by segment

Segment Functional job Social job Emotional job
Young families When going out means babysitters and $300 nights, help me bring the good night home so the social life survives the kids. When my mates come over, help me host without effort so my place stays the default. Help me feel like having kids didn't end the fun — it just moved the venue.
High-volume drinkers When my weekly bottle-shop run costs $100+, help me get better beer for less so the habit stops stinging. (44 schooners per 20L keg, $120–170.) When I shout the boys, help me pour like a publican instead of handing out tins. Help me feel smart about what I drink — fresher, cheaper, sorted — not guilty about it.
Cashed-up tradies When the shed build is nearly done, help me finish it with the piece everyone talks about. When mates see the setup, help me be the bloke whose shed has beer on tap. Help me feel the build — and the money — was worth it.
Baby boomers When the family descends on weekends, help me keep everyone poured without playing waiter. When we host, help me entertain generously — proper beer for the boys, seltzer for the granddaughters. Help me feel like the hub the family gathers around.
Share houses When four of us split everything, help us own the party asset no other house has. Help our place be the one people show up to. Help us feel like the $975 four ways was the easiest money we ever spent.
DINKs When we'd rather two great drinks than a fridge of average ones, help us pour top-shelf and cocktails at home. Help our place read as considered — the good stuff, on tap. Help us feel taste is something we own, not order.
Workplaces When Friday knock-offs deserve better than a car-park slab, help us make the office worth staying at. Help the boss look like the boss. Help the team feel looked after.
Partner / veto-holder When he wants a $975 beer system, help me see what's in it for the whole household. (~1/3 of 27 taps non-beer; gluten-reduced, lower-carb.) When people are over, help me host well too — gin fizz and seltzer on tap, not just his lager. Help me feel this was our good decision, not his toy.
Existing system owners When my keg's empty, help me get it filled fresh, fast and consistently — whoever sold me the hardware. Help me be the owner whose system always pours well. Help me feel the system was a good call after all.

The pattern: every primary segment's social job is a variant of make my place the default venue. That is the campaign-level job — the pillars (kudos, connection) are just its two faces, and cost is the justification that follows (Christy's dirty-little-secret rule).

Jobs by purchase moment

The four moments hire Tap That Brewery for different things. Conflating them produces creative that sells everything and closes nothing.

Moment The job being hired Success looks like Fired when
System purchase ($975–$2,550, at cost) Upgrade my home into the venue. A considered household identity purchase — closer to a barbecue or spa than to beer The build finished, the first pour in front of mates, the partner on side The barriers win: upfront cost, veto, space, self-trust (see 07)
Refill (~$200/month; the margin engine) Never let the tap run dry, without thinking about it. A convenience/habit job — logistics, not identity Always something good pouring; the swap is effortless (plastic pick-up-and-go; delivery pending) The errand outweighs the habit: 25-minute round trips, corny Tue–Fri wait, "haven't gotten around to it" drift past 90 days
Taproom visit Show me the life before I buy it. For locals: a good, cheap session (Knockoffs happy hour, $18–20 paddle). For prospects: the live demo The "that's a keg system?" moment — 20–30% of visitors become owners It's just another brewery visit: no demo beat, no follow-up capturing the 3-month window
Membership (Keg Crew $250/yr) Make my habit pay and make me a regular. $30 off every refill (repays in ~4 months at 2 kegs/month), 100 Tap Tokens, 5L birthday keg Belonging with maths behind it — leaderboard, tasting nights, mugs at schooner prices The value is unclear (two conflicting posters) or unused (lapsed refill cadence makes the fee feel wasted)

The system linking them: the taproom sells the dream, the system purchase buys the identity, refills are the habit the margin lives on, and membership is the contract with the habit. Each moment's creative should do its own job — a refill SMS never needs to resell the dream; a system campaign should never lead with a refill discount.

Assumptions & open items

  • JTBD statements are Jewell constructions on client-stated segment logic and objections — pressure-test the wording against census verbatims and real customer conversations before they anchor Design-phase copy.
  • Membership payback maths uses the newer poster ($250/yr, $30/refill) — pending the pricing reconciliation flagged in D06.
  • The veto-holder jobs assume the partner is beer-averse; census may show a share who are direct customers of the non-beer taps in their own right — which would upgrade her from veto-holder to co-buyer in the messaging.

Feeds into: DS01 Customer Profile, DS02 Customer Journey, DS04 Brand Strategy (message architecture), segment campaign briefs (DS-phase).

Competitor Analysis

Competitor groups (by type, not just name)

Type Who What they sell Where Tap That Brewery wins
Local keg-refill breweries Aardvark & Arrow* (literally across the road), Laughing Lizard (northern Gold Coast) Similar keg/refill model, cheaper kegs ($85–90 vs $120–170) Quality + consistency, award-backed brewing, 27-tap variety incl. non-beer, membership/loyalty ecosystem, the taproom as demo floor
Online keg distributors Kegs Off Tap, Keg Republic Commodity kegs shipped, wholesale positioning Freshness (20L brewed on site), local relationship, service (Tue-drop/Fri-ready corny turnaround), community
Bottleshops + big retail Dan Murphy's et al. Packaged beer convenience Price per litre (no excise, no wholesaler), freshness, the experience of tap at home
Pubs/venues Local hospitality The night out Cost ("a week's trade in your garage"), no babysitter, host status
Locked channel CUB-exclusive NRL venues Tap contracts Not winnable head-on — route around via non-affiliated clubs and white-label

* Spelling per handwritten workshop note ("across the road"); Circleback transcribed "Ardbach and Arrow." Confirm name and profile properly before Final.

The two axes where Tap That Brewery can lead

  1. Freshness & range (20L brewed-here variety + 27 taps incl. RTDs/seltzers) vs commodity 50L distribution.
  2. Ecosystem & belonging (memberships, Tap Tokens, leaderboard, referral rewards, giveaways, tours, functions) vs transactional keg swaps.

Price is deliberately not an axis — Tap That Brewery is 40–90% dearer per keg than local rivals and must never fight on sticker price; it fights on per-litre economics vs retail/pub and on everything else vs keg rivals.

Contrast lines (draft)

  • Unlike the online keg warehouses, Tap That Brewery brews what you pour — 20L at a time, fresh, five minutes from where you drink it.
  • Unlike the cheap keg swap across the road, Tap That Brewery is a range: 27 taps, a third of them for the people who don't drink beer.
  • Unlike the pub, your garage doesn't have a closing time — or a babysitter surcharge.

Category gap + proof

Gap: nobody is building the installed-base refill brand — every competitor sells kegs; none owns the relationship with the Gold Coast's existing keg system owners regardless of where the hardware came from. Proof it works: ~50% of Tap That Brewery's refill customers switched from other suppliers with no targeted campaign; ~70% of corny-keg holdouts converted to Tap That Brewery's plastic system once they experienced it.

Benchmark to beat: Aardvark & Arrow — same suburb, same model, lower price. If the strategy can't articulate why a refill customer crosses the road toward Tap That Brewery, it isn't finished.

One-sentence position (draft)

Tap That Brewery is the Gold Coast's home-tap brand: award-winning beer and a 27-tap range, brewed in Burleigh, filling any keg system — because good times at your place beat a night out anywhere else.

Feeds into: D06 synthesis, DS04 Brand Strategy. Pending: verified competitor names, pricing sweep, search-visibility scan.

Category Positioning
Document Category Positioning — catalogue #10
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects
Builds on D03 Competitor Analysis, D06 Discovery Pack Synthesis

Purpose

D03 identified who Tap That Brewery competes with. This document fixes where the brand stands relative to each competitor type, names the two axes it can lead on, and states why price is deliberately excluded as a battleground. It is the positioning input for DS04 Brand Strategy — proposed, not yet decided.

The category map: five competitor types, five different fights

Tap That Brewery does not have one competitor set — it has five, and the winning argument is different against each. Positioning fails if one message is asked to beat all five.

Type Who What the customer is really buying from them The fight Tap That Brewery picks
Local keg-refill breweries Aardvark & Arrow* (literally across the road, $85–90/keg), Laughing Lizard (northern GC) The same model, cheaper Range + ecosystem: 27 taps, award-backed brewing, membership/Tap Tokens/leaderboard, the taproom as a working demo floor. Never sticker price.
Online keg distributors Kegs Off Tap, Keg Republic Commodity 50L kegs shipped from a warehouse Freshness + relationship: 20L brewed in Burleigh, corny kegs dropped Tuesday and ready Friday, a venue you can walk into.
Bottleshops / big retail Dan Murphy's et al. Packaged convenience Per-litre economics (no excise under threshold, no wholesaler margin: ~$275 equivalent vs ~$500 for a 50L Balter through a wholesaler) plus the experience of tap at home.
Pubs / venues Local hospitality The night out Cost and freedom: no babysitter, no closing time, no $12 schooners. The garage wins.
Locked channel CUB-exclusive NRL-affiliated venues Contracted taps Not winnable head-on. Route around: non-affiliated clubs via white-label ("Surfers Paradise Demons Lager", 10%/10%), weddings, function venues.

* Name unverified — handwritten workshop note reads "Aardvark and Arrow (across the road)"; the Circleback transcript heard "Ardbach and Arrow." Every use of this name in this document carries that caveat until verified.

The structural point: only the first row sells what Tap That Brewery sells. Rows two to four are where the customers currently are; row one is where the refill margin gets contested. Positioning must recruit from bottleshops and pubs while defending against the keg swap across the road — two different jobs.

The two axes Tap That Brewery can lead on

Axis 1 — Freshness & range. 20L kegs brewed on site versus the industry's commodity 50L format; 27 taps with roughly a third deliberately non-beer (seltzers, RTDs, cocktails, ginger beer); a first-year Brewer's Choice award at Crafted (Midnight in Tokyo) as third-party proof. No local rival can claim the range breadth, and no online distributor can claim the freshness — this axis beats both at once.

Axis 2 — Ecosystem & belonging. Keg Crew membership, Tap Tokens with a public leaderboard (top holder 7,569 points as of 1/8/26), referral rewards (1,000 tokens per system referral), 2×/year giveaways drawing ~1,000 entries, brewery tours, functions, and a taproom that converts 20–30% of visitors into system owners. Competitors run keg swaps; Tap That Brewery runs a club. "We're in the business of connection and good times" is the owner's own sentence — this axis is that sentence operationalised.

Everything else (service turnaround, delivery, Benchy) hangs off these two. If a piece of creative can't be traced to freshness/range or ecosystem/belonging, it is off-position.

Why price is deliberately not an axis

Tap That Brewery's kegs are $120–170 against $85–90 across the road — 40–90% dearer. A price fight against local rivals is unwinnable and would poison the quality claim the award underwrites. But cost is a pillar — the resolution is targeting, not denial:

  • Against retail and pubs, cost leads on Tap That Brewery's terms: price per litre, no excise, no wholesaler, ~$275 vs ~$500. That maths is unassailable and doesn't mention the shop across the road.
  • Against keg rivals, cost is never mentioned; freshness, range and ecosystem carry the argument.
  • Per Christy's working rule (adopted in D06): cost is the customer's dirty little secret — kudos and connection do the buying, savings justify it afterwards. Cost closes; it never opens.

One-sentence position (carried from D03, confirmed against the map)

Tap That Brewery is the Gold Coast's home-tap brand: award-winning beer and a 27-tap range, brewed in Burleigh, filling any keg system — because good times at your place beat a night out anywhere else.

Read against the map: "award-winning… 27-tap range, brewed in Burleigh" is Axis 1 aimed at keg rivals and distributors; "filling any keg system" claims the installed base (see #11 Market Gaps); "good times at your place beat a night out" recruits from pubs and retail on Axis 2 without a price number in sight.

Assumptions & open items

  • Competitor name unverified — "Aardvark & Arrow" vs "Ardbach and Arrow"; confirm spelling, pricing and profile before any external use.
  • Competitor pricing ($85–90) is the client's report, not a verified sweep; Laughing Lizard unprofiled; no search-visibility scan yet.
  • The 40–90% premium calculation assumes like-for-like 20L comparison — confirm rival keg sizes.
  • Position sentence is a Discover proposal; DS04 Brand Strategy decides.

Feeds into: #11 Market Gaps, #12 Differentiation Opportunities, D06 synthesis (market section), DS04 Brand Strategy, D08 Gate 1 Deck.

Document 11 of 65

Market Gaps
Document Market Gaps — catalogue #11
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects
Builds on D03 Competitor Analysis, #10 Category Positioning, D06 Discovery Pack Synthesis

Purpose

Five gaps surfaced in discovery that no competitor — local, online, or retail — currently owns. For each: the evidence it exists, the signal of its size, and what claiming it would actually take. These are opportunity hypotheses for Design-phase prioritisation, not commitments.

Gap 1 — The installed-base refill relationship ("we fill any system")

The category's structural blind spot. Every competitor sells kegs; nobody owns the relationship with the Gold Coast's existing keg system owners — the garages and backyards that need filling monthly regardless of who sold the hardware. This is the white space D06 made quotable: nobody owns "beer on tap at home" on the Gold Coast.
Evidence ~50% of Tap That Brewery's 220 refill customers bought their system elsewhere and switched — with zero targeted campaign. ~70% of corny-keg holdouts converted to the plastic bladder system once they experienced it.
Size signal If half the current base arrived organically, the addressable installed base is plausibly several multiples of 220. It is the discovery session's stated "lowest-hanging fruit," and D06's H1 hypothesises switchers beat new-owner acquisition on CAC — no $975 objection, no 3-month buying cycle, margin from the first fill.
What claiming it takes A named "we fill any system" campaign lane (currently the offer exists but is unspoken); a first-fill switch incentive (Tap Tokens or member-rate first keg); corny Tue→Fri turnaround as the service proof; landing page + Meta/local targeting at homebrew and keg-owner audiences. Low capex — the constraint is Raef's ~70% marketing allocation.

Gap 2 — The portable/outdoor tap category (Benchy — zero competitors)
Evidence The Benchy portable unit (Kegland hardware) targets 4WD, camping, boating and caravan use — discovery's flat statement: no direct competitors currently. Raef's cousin runs a 4WD fit-out business and has confirmed client interest. The Smoked Barbecue Co Father's Day collab already proves the adjacent outdoor-living partnership model.
Size signal Qualitative only (one channel contact, no sales data) — but category creation, not share-stealing: 4WD/outdoor retail reaches buyers with money for lifestyle rigs, 6–9 months ahead of purchase intent, in channels no keg rival occupies. Every Benchy is a new refill account in a segment competitors can't see.
What claiming it takes Productise it (pricing, bundle, name it on the price card — currently absent); one flagship fit-out partnership via the existing 4WD contact; content built for the segment (rig installs, campsite pours — UGC-native); a decision on refill logistics for customers who roam (5L format and delivery both help). Modest effort, but it needs an owner or it stays an anecdote.

Gap 3 — The health-conscious range (gluten-reduced, lower-carb — unmarketed)
Evidence The beers are gluten-reduced (cannot legally claim gluten-free) and the range skews lower-carb by design; discovery confirms the angle exists but isn't yet marketed — it appears nowhere in venue signage or socials. The census (n=50) shows the customer is a moderator, not a binger: 70% use their system daily/most days yet drink only 1–2 kegs/month.
Size signal D06's H7 flags it as a differentiator for the female market (the veto-holder on the #1 objection, "my wife won't let me") and moderating drinkers. No local keg rival or distributor claims health credentials — a first-mover claim that costs copy, not capex.
What claiming it takes Compliance first: lock the permissible wording ("gluten-reduced," carb claims) before any copy ships. Then: tap decals/menu flags, a website range page, and the "fresher, better, not more" moderation frame from D02. Cheapest gap on this list to claim — and the easiest for a competitor to copy once spoken, so speed matters.

Gap 4 — White-label club and wedding beer
Evidence The model is already designed: supply the system, white-label the beer ("Surfers Paradise Demons Lager"), 10% player discount + 10% club kickback. Wedding and function venues named in discovery as white-label wholesale targets. Sports clubs currently rebuff cans-and-contracts pitches (no packaged product, existing supplier deals, no tap infrastructure); NRL-affiliated venues are CUB-locked.
Size signal Every club or venue converted is a standing multi-keg refill account, not a one-off — wholesale exists to feed refill volume. The 10%/10% structure converts sponsorship-fatigued clubs by making the club a stakeholder, an offer neither CUB nor the online distributors is structured to make.
What claiming it takes A one-page club offer (mock decal, the 10%/10% maths); target only non-affiliated clubs with tap infrastructure or bundle the system in; fix the follow-up leak D06 names — DM outreach gets responses but pipeline dies between first reply and manager contact. Needs a tracked pipeline more than it needs marketing.

Gap 5 — Home delivery
Evidence Census-validated demand: respondents accept a $20–30 fee and 3–7 day wait. GoTab integrates with Uber Direct for same-day. No local keg rival delivers; online distributors ship freight, not fresh.
Size signal Convenience is a stated brand pillar the current model under-delivers (pickup only, corny turnaround excepted). Delivery widens the geographic refill radius beyond Burleigh, extends keg cadence for at-risk customers (75–90 days) who "hadn't gotten around to coming in," and is the fulfilment layer Gaps 1 and 2 both lean on.
What claiming it takes GoTab × Uber Direct integration switched on (pending); a priced pilot ($20–30 per census); a delivery radius decision; positioning as fresh, local, to your door — the one claim warehouse distributors cannot make. Blocked on the same integration backlog as lifecycle automation — sequencing decision for Design.

Priority read (proposed)

Gap 1 is the strategy — cheapest CAC, direct line to the 1,000-customer target. Gap 3 is the fastest claim (copy only, compliance permitting). Gaps 4 and 5 are gated by pipeline discipline and the GoTab integration respectively. Gap 2 is the defensible long game: the only gap where Tap That Brewery wouldn't just lead the category but be the category.

Assumptions & open items

  • All size signals are directional — no market sizing exists for GC keg-system installed base, 4WD accessory demand, or club venue counts.
  • Census figures are headline-only (n=50, full data pending); delivery fee tolerance rests on it.
  • Gluten-reduced/low-carb claims need compliance wording locked before use.
  • Benchy pricing and margin unconfirmed; competitor absence is the client's claim, not a verified scan.
  • Competitor name "Aardvark & Arrow" unverified (transcript: "Ardbach and Arrow").

Feeds into: #12 Differentiation Opportunities, DS03 Marketing Plan (campaign lanes + sequencing), DS04 Brand Strategy, D08 Gate 1 Deck.

Differentiation Opportunities
Document Differentiation Opportunities — catalogue #12
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects
Builds on D03 Competitor Analysis, #10 Category Positioning, #11 Market Gaps, D05 Offer Worksheet

Purpose

Six differentiators, each with proof already in hand — no aspiration, no "we believe." For each: the evidence, the contrast line it earns, and where it should show up (web, in-venue, campaigns). Input to DS04 Brand Strategy and all Design-phase copy; contrast lines are drafts for voice work, not final copy.

The rule

A differentiator without proof is a slogan. Every entry below survives D03's test: backed by something photographed in venue, said by the owner, or counted in the data — not drawn so Tap That Brewery wins by definition.

The six differentiators

1. Award-winning brewing

  • Proof: Midnight in Tokyo (black Japanese lager, 4.6%) won Brewer's Choice at Crafted — described as Australia's biggest beer festival — in Tap That Brewery's first year entering. Head brewer Chris Smith's pedigree (ex Burleigh Barrels, 4 Pines venues) documented in the Gold Coast Bulletin founding article.
  • Contrast line: Unlike the keg swap across the road, the beer in your keg took Brewer's Choice at Australia's biggest beer festival — first year we entered.
  • Where it shows up: Web — homepage trust bar + Midnight in Tokyo product page. In-venue — award signage at the tap and on the takeaway price board (currently absent). Campaigns — the justifier in every switcher ad; the answer to "why pay $120 when it's $85 over there."

2. 20L freshness vs 50L commodity

  • Proof: 20L brewed on site against the industry-standard 50L distribution format; the customer watches the fermenters from the bar rail. Smaller format means faster turnover and more variety per system.
  • Contrast line: Unlike the online keg warehouses, Tap That Brewery brews what you pour — 20L at a time, fresh, five minutes from where you drink it.
  • Where it shows up: Web — "why 20L" explainer on the refills page. In-venue — the brewhouse is the display; coolroom "Kegged & Ready / Crafting Soon" board turned into content. Campaigns — the core anti-distributor message; "brewed this week" freshness-dating worth testing.

3. 27-tap range including non-beer

  • Proof: 27 taps, ~1/3 deliberately non-beer — seltzers, RTDs, gin sours, cocktails, ginger beer (photo-verified tap wall). Engineered to defeat the #1 objection ("my wife won't let me"): her drinks on tap too. Gluten-reduced, lower-carb range as unmarketed backup (see #11, Gap 3).
  • Contrast line: Unlike the cheap keg swap, Tap That Brewery is a range: 27 taps, a third of them for the people who don't drink beer.
  • Where it shows up: Web — full tap list with ABVs and non-beer filter. In-venue — already the hero; add "a third of these aren't beer" framing. Campaigns — the partner-buy-in beat in every young-family and tradie ad ("and here's what's on tap for her").

4. The membership/Tap Tokens/leaderboard ecosystem

  • Proof: Keg Crew membership ($250/yr: $30 off every refill, 100 tokens, 5L birthday keg — pricing reconciliation pending, two conflicting posters); Tap Tokens on every dollar with a public leaderboard (Jack P. leading on 7,569 points, 1/8/26); referral rewards (1,000 tokens per system referral, 500 per refill referral); giveaways drawing ~1,000 entries. Competitors run transactions; nobody else has a leaderboard.
  • Contrast line: Unlike a keg supplier, Tap That Brewery is a club you're winning — tokens, leaderboard, birthday keg and all.
  • Where it shows up: Web — membership page (post-reconciliation) + live leaderboard. In-venue — already strong; make Keg Crew the default at system purchase, not an upsell. Campaigns — retention and referral engine; leaderboard and token milestones as recurring social content.

5. Corny keg service: Tue drop → Fri ready

  • Proof: Photo-verified sign: corny kegs dropped by COB Tuesday, cleaned ($3) and filled by Friday. A concrete service promise no warehouse shipper structurally can match — and the front door for the "we fill any system" claim (see #11, Gap 1), since corny owners are, by definition, people who bought hardware elsewhere.
  • Contrast line: Unlike the distributors, we don't care whose gear you own — drop your keg Tuesday, pour our beer Friday.
  • Where it shows up: Web — "we fill any system" page with the Tue→Fri promise as the mechanism. In-venue — existing sign, keep. Campaigns — the switcher campaign's working proof point; the weekend framing ("ready for Friday") writes its own creative.

6. No-excise price-per-litre economics

  • Proof: Under the excise threshold, no wholesaler margin: ~$275 equivalent vs ~$500 for a 50L Balter through a wholesaler. A 20L keg is 44 schooners for $120–170 against $10–18 a schooner at a venue.
  • Contrast line: Unlike the pub, your garage doesn't have a closing time — or a babysitter surcharge. And the beer costs half as much a litre.
  • Where it shows up: Web — a savings calculator on the refills page (kegs/month → dollars vs bottle-o and pub). In-venue — nowhere prominent; keep it that way, cost is the closer, not the opener. Campaigns — retargeting and close-stage only, per the dirty-little-secret rule; leads openly only for the high-volume segment (D02).

Deployment summary

Differentiator Web In-venue Campaigns Sequencing note
Award-winning brewing Trust bar Add signage Every switcher ad Ready now
20L freshness Explainer Brewhouse visible Anti-distributor lead Ready now
27-tap range Tap list Hero already Partner-buy-in beat Ready now
Ecosystem Membership page Strong already Retention/referral Blocked: price reconciliation
Corny Tue→Fri "Fill any system" page Signed already Switcher proof Ready now
Price-per-litre Calculator Deliberately quiet Close-stage only Ready now

Note the asymmetry: in-venue already carries most of this story (which is why the taproom converts 20–30% of visitors); the web carries almost none of it. The differentiation gap is not message — it is channel: the 3-month buying cycle happens away from the venue, where none of these proofs are currently visible.

Assumptions & open items

  • Membership pricing must be reconciled ($250/yr vs $300+$120 renewal posters) before differentiator 4 ships anywhere.
  • Competitor references ("across the road", $85–90) rest on the unverified Aardvark & Arrow name and client-reported pricing. Transcript: "Ardbach and Arrow" — confirm before external use.*
  • "Australia's biggest beer festival" is the client's description of Crafted — verify before print.
  • Contrast lines are strategy drafts, not final copy; voice pass happens in Design.
  • Current website content not yet audited — "web carries almost none of it" is inferred from discovery, confirm with a site review.

Feeds into: DS04 Brand Strategy (messaging hierarchy), DS03 Marketing Plan (channel deployment), website brief, campaign creative briefs, D08 Gate 1 Deck.

The membership maths does the retention work: at 2 refills/month, Keg Crew's $30-per-refill discount repays $250 in ~4 months — a member who stays active is cheaper per keg, and a lapsed member is visible in the data.

Expansion offers

  • Benchy portable tap — 4WD/camping/boating/caravan; no direct competitor.
  • Delivery — census-validated ($20–30 fee, 3–7 days acceptable); GoTab × Uber Direct enables same-day.
  • White-label wholesale — sports clubs ("Surfers Paradise Demons Lager": 10% player discount, 10% club kickback), wedding/function venues.
  • Functions — mezzanine (~40) to full venue (~120–130 seated); minimum-spend model (1 drink/person/hour benchmark).

The re-education pathway (moving buyers up the tiers)

Taproom visit → tasting paddle → "that's a keg system?" moment (20–30% of visitors convert to owners) → giveaway entry → nurture across the 3-month buying cycle → 50%-off close or lease-to-buy → Keg Crew membership at purchase → refill cadence + tokens + referrals.

Gap: this pathway exists physically in the venue but has no owned digital equivalent — the 3-month consideration window is currently unmanaged.

One-line offer (draft)

Beer on tap at home for less than the bottle-o — the system at cost, the good times on subscription.

Strengths and gaps

All product pricing runs on a three-band structure: Core / Premium / Top Shelf.

5L mini system $225 —

Afterpay + Zip accepted; lease-to-buy in development (GoTab-dependent). The bonus credit is quietly smart packaging: it forces the first refill transaction, which is where the actual business begins.

Memberships — two conflicting live posters (production gate)

Older poster Newer poster
Keg Crew $300 join + $120/yr renewal · 100 tokens $250/yr · 100 tokens · $30 off keg refills · 5L birthday keg · mugs at schooner prices · tasting nights + free drink · 10% off Never Quit Gym
Mug Club $120/yr · 50 tokens $120/yr · 75 tokens
Brew Buds Free · 10 tokens · incl. Taps & Tales tour tickets Free = mailing list · 50 tokens

Reconciliation required before Gate 1 — pricing, token schedule and inclusions all differ, and the keg systems card independently lists Keg Crew at $250/yr (supporting the newer poster). Until the client confirms which is live, all strategy work assumes the newer poster.

Competitive price position

Comparison Them Tap That Brewery
Local keg rivals (Aardvark & Arrow*, Laughing Lizard) $85–90/keg $120–170 (20L core–top shelf, non-member $150–170 for core/premium)
Wholesale route (50L Balter via wholesaler) ~$500 ~$275 equivalent (no excise under threshold, no wholesaler margin)
Bottle-shop / pub per serve pub schooner economics 20L core member keg = $120 ÷ ~44 schooners ≈ $2.70/schooner at home

The read (consistent with D03): Tap That Brewery is 40–90% dearer than local keg rivals and must never fight them on sticker price — the fight is quality, range, ecosystem. Against retail, pubs and the wholesale route, the per-litre maths is the weapon — and per Christy's working rule, it closes rather than leads.

What the packaging logic is actually doing

  1. Hardware at cost = a paid lead. The $975–$2,550 system buys a customer, not margin; Afterpay/Zip and (soon) lease-to-buy exist purely to shrink the entry objection.
  2. The member/non-member spread is the retention device. $30 off every 20L refill is exactly the Keg Crew discount — at the observed ~2 refills/month cadence, $250/yr repays in ~4 months, after which the member is economically locked in and their lapse is visible in the data.
  3. Top Shelf is a kudos band, not a volume band. $345–375 20L kegs and $70 growlers price the status buyer ("cashed-up wankers", DINKs) without touching core affordability.
  4. Happy hour and the paddle are funnel pricing — cheap trial serves that exist to trigger the "that's a keg system?" moment, not to make taproom margin.
  5. The 5L tier is the gift/gateway rung ($225 system, $75–155 refills, birthday keg perk) — the lowest-risk on-ramp to ownership.

Assumptions & open items

  • Membership poster reconciliation (blocking); paddle $18 vs $20.
  • Refill margin per keg not yet quantified — needed to model member-discount economics properly (the ~4-month payback is a revenue-side calculation).
  • Competitor keg size for the $85–90 price point unconfirmed (assumed comparable); competitor name spelling unverified*.
  • Benchy, keg hire and venue hire all POA/unpriced — packaging opportunity, not yet designed.
  • Lease-to-buy terms and timing (GoTab, "couple of months").

* Per handwritten workshop note; Circleback transcribed "Ardbach and Arrow".

Sales Process Review
Document Sales Process Review — catalogue #16
Engagement Jewell Projects × Tap That Brewery
Phase 01 Discover
Status Draft v01 — Internal
Date 19 August 2026
Prepared by Jewell Projects

Purpose. How systems and refills actually get sold today — the real mechanics, with their conversion numbers where known — and the named gaps. The headline: Tap That Brewery converts remarkably well at the moments it controls, and loses buyers in every gap between those moments.

How a keg system sells today

Route 1 — the taproom demo (always on)

Visitor drinks in the venue → sees the working brewhouse and tap wall → "that's a keg system?" moment → 20–30% of taproom visitors go on to buy a system (client estimate). Supporting props: the "Did you say beer on tap at home?" flyer (pillars printed: Cost–Convenience–Connection–Kudos; home/workplace/sports club/restaurant framing), demo bookings via cheers@tapthatbrewery.com.au.

What's missing: no capture. A visitor who doesn't buy on the day leaves anonymous — no name, no follow-up trigger, nothing that acknowledges the buying cycle below.

Route 2 — the giveaway funnel (2×/year, the volume driver)

Keg-system giveaway competition → ~1,000 entries per cycle → winner drawn → all entrants receive a 50%-off system offer at competition close → 22–25 systems sold last cycle. That is the business's single biggest sales event: roughly 2.2–2.5% entry-to-purchase, twice a year.

What's missing: the other ~975 entrants — self-identified keg-system wanters with contact details — receive nothing between cycles. The list is the asset; the offer is the only touch.

The consideration window nobody manages

The buying cycle is long: ~10% buy within a month, ~60% take up to 3 months, 40% longer. Four recurring objections, each with a live response:

Objection Current answer
Upfront cost (~$975) At-cost pricing story, Afterpay/Zip, lease-to-buy in development (GoTab-dependent), 50%-off giveaway close
"My wife won't let me" 1/3 non-beer taps (seltzers, RTDs, cocktails) — her drinks on tap too
Nowhere to put it Mini 5L system ($225), integrated options
"I'll drink too much" Census (n=50): 70% of daily users go through only 1–2 kegs/month; "put a lock on the fridge"

But the window itself is unmanaged: no email/SMS nurture, no objection-answering content sequence, no retargeting. A 3-month decision is currently nurtured by the buyer's memory of a good afternoon at the taproom.

How refills sell today

  • Cadence: active customers average ~2 kegs/month (~$200/month). Plastic bladder = walk in, swap, go; corny = drop COB Tuesday, ready Friday.
  • Retention device: Keg Crew membership ($30 off every refill — repays ~$250/yr in ~4 months at cadence) plus Tap Tokens on every dollar, leaderboard, referral rewards (1,000 tokens for a system or venue referral, 500 for a refill referral).
  • Lifecycle: active <75 days since last keg · at-risk 75–90 · churned 90+.
  • Winback: a manual monthly SMS to churned customers — $20 off if they buy within 2 weeks; this month testing a free family-sized Marie's Pizza (Buttercard partnership). Automation waits on the GoTab/Fishbowl integration. Churned customers contacted personally mostly say they've cut back or "hadn't gotten around to it" — lapsed, not lost.
  • Switchers: ~50% of refill customers bought hardware elsewhere and switched to Tap That Brewery for fills — with zero targeted effort. There is no active sales process for this, the cheapest acquisition lane the business has.

Wholesale outreach history

Approach Result
Walk-ins (Broadbeach restaurants) Failed — never reached decision-makers, couldn't showcase the range
Cold email / calls Failed — same decision-maker problem
Style-matched targeting (Japanese lager → Japanese/Asian fusion; Mexican lager → Mexican; craft → US-style bars) The right idea — sharpened relevance
Social media DMs Best response rate — venues passed on a manager's contact…
Follow-up on those leads …and follow-up was admittedly loose — pipeline died between first reply and manager contact

Sports clubs hit three objections — no packaged cans, existing supplier contracts, no tap infrastructure — plus NRL-affiliated venues locked by CUB exclusivity. The white-label play (club-branded lager, 10% player discount / 10% club kickback) is the designed answer, not yet systematised.

The process gaps, named

  1. No taproom lead capture — the highest-converting moment (20–30%) has no mechanism to hold the 70–80% who don't buy that day.
  2. The giveaway list dies between cycles — ~1,000 warm, self-qualified leads twice a year get one offer and then silence.
  3. The 3-month window is unmanaged — no nurture matched to the known objections and known cycle length (D06 hypothesis H3 targets exactly this).
  4. No switcher process — the channel producing half the refill base has no campaign, no offer, no landing page.
  5. Wholesale has no pipeline — DM leads leak for want of a simple CRM stage list and follow-up cadence.
  6. Winback is manual and post-churn — the $20-off SMS fires after 90 days; nothing touches the at-risk 75–90 window (hypothesis H4).
  7. Referrals are signage, not process — Tap Token rewards exist on a wall poster; nobody is asked at the moments of highest goodwill (system handover, giveaway win, great refill visit).

Assumptions & open items

  • 20–30% taproom conversion is a client estimate — validate against GoTab data once available.
  • Giveaway figures are last-cycle recall (22–25 sales from ~1,000 entries) — confirm exact counts and entry-list ownership/consent status.
  • Winback SMS response rate unknown; Marie's Pizza test unmeasured as yet.
  • GoTab/Fishbowl integration timeline — blocking for all automation design.

Feeds into: #17 Funnel Review, D06 synthesis, DS02 Customer Journey, DS03 Marketing Plan, DS11 Sales-on-a-Page.
